Appendix A
Convention

In this appendix we collect our conventions used throughout the paper and the appendices. The metric signature is
\eta_{\mu\nu}={\rm diag}[+1,-1,-1,-1]
. We absorb factors of
2\pi
in the integration measure and the delta functions as

\hat{\mathrm{d}}^{n}p:=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}p}{(2\pi)^{n}}\,,\quad\hat{\delta}^{n}(p):=(2\pi)^{n}\delta^{n}(p)\,.

(25)

The Lorentz-invariant phase-space measure is denoted by

\displaystyle d\Phi(p_{1},p_{2},\cdots):=\prod_{a}\hat{\rm d}^{4}p_{a}\hat{\delta}(p_{a}^{2}-M_{a}^{2})\theta(p^{0}_{a})=\prod_{a}\frac{\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}_{a}}{2E_{a}}\,,

(26)

and an on-shell one-particle state is normalized as

\displaystyle\bra{p'_1}\ket{p_1}=\hat{\delta}_{\Phi}(p^{\prime}_{1}-p_{1}):=2E_{1}\hat{\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}-\bm{p}_{1})\,,

(27)

with the conventional in-out notation adopted. The free states are denoted by using their labels, and semicolons are used to separate the labels of different particles, e.g.,
\ket{p_1,s_1;k}=\ket{p_1,s_1}\otimes\ket{k}
.

In the spinor-helicity formalism, it is customary to use the all-incoming (or all-outgoing) notation. We adopt the all-incoming notation, so
p^{0}>0
denotes an incoming particle and
p^{0}<0
is an outgoing particle, respectively. This requires flipping the signs of the momentum and the helicity when dealing with outgoing states. One should not confuse the bra-ket notation of the states with the bra-ket of the spinors explained below. The difference between the two should be clear from the context. The Pauli matrices are

\displaystyle(\sigma^{\mu})_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=(\bm{1},\sigma^{i})_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\,,\quad(\bar{\sigma}^{\mu})^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}=(\bm{1},-\sigma^{i})^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}

(28)

with

\displaystyle\sigma^{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\sigma^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\sigma^{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\\
\end{pmatrix}\,.

(29)

The spinor indices are raised and lowered by the Levi-Civita tensor,
\epsilon
, acting from “left”:

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}=\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}=-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}=-\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,,\quad\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\epsilon^{\beta\gamma}=\delta_{\alpha}^{\gamma}\,.

(30)

We adopt the same rules for raising and lowering indices of the
SU(2)
little group. In the spinor-helicity language, the four-momenta become

\displaystyle p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}p_{\mu}\,,\quad p^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}=p_{\mu}(\bar{\sigma}^{\mu})^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\,,

(31)

with the explicit massless/massive spinors being

\displaystyle\text{massless:}\ p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha}\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\,,

(32)

\displaystyle\text{massive:}\ p_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{I}_{\alpha}\tilde{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\,.

(33)

We relate spinors with
p
and
-p
via

\displaystyle\lambda(-p)=\lambda(p)\,,\quad\tilde{\lambda}(-p)=-\tilde{\lambda}(p)\,.

(34)

For real kinematics, we have

\displaystyle\text{massless:}\ (\lambda_{\alpha})^{*}

\displaystyle={\rm sign}(p^{0})\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\,,

(35)

\displaystyle\text{massive:}\ (\lambda^{I}_{\alpha})^{*}

\displaystyle={\rm sign}(p^{0})\tilde{\lambda}_{I\dot{\alpha}}\,,\ (\lambda_{I\alpha})^{*}=-{\rm sign}(p^{0})\tilde{\lambda}^{I}_{\dot{\alpha}}\,.

(36)

In the bra-ket notation, the spinors are represented by

\displaystyle\ket*{p}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\lambda_{\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\quad\bra*{p}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\lambda^{\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\quad|p]

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,

\displaystyle\quad[q|

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\,,

(37)

\displaystyle\ket*{p^I}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\lambda^{I}_{\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\quad\bra*{p^I}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\lambda^{I\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\quad|p^{I}]

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\tilde{\lambda}^{I\dot{\alpha}}\,,

\displaystyle\quad[q^{I}|

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\tilde{\lambda^{I}}_{\dot{\alpha}}\,.

(38)

For concrete calculations, we can use the following realizations of massive and massless ingoing spinors:

\displaystyle\ket*{p_a^I}=\sqrt{M_{a}}\,e^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}(\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\sigma})}\,,\quad|p_{a}^{I}]=\sqrt{M_{a}}\,e^{+\frac{\lambda}{2}(\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\sigma})}\,,

(39)

\displaystyle|k\rangle=\sqrt{2\omega}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-e^{-i\varphi/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\\
e^{i\varphi/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,,\ |k]=\sqrt{2\omega}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e^{-i\varphi/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\\
e^{i\varphi/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,,

(40)

for momenta
p^{\mu}_{a}=(M_{a}\cosh\lambda,M_{a}\sinh\lambda\bm{n})
where
\bm{\sigma}
being the usual three-vector of Pauli matrices, and massless momentum
k^{\mu}=\omega(1,\cos\varphi\sin\theta,\sin\varphi\sin\theta,\cos\theta)
. Here, one can use the formula involving an exponentiation of the Pauli matrices

\displaystyle e^{i\theta(\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\sigma})}=\bm{1}\cos\theta+i\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\sigma}\sin\theta\,,

(41)

with
\theta=\pm\frac{i}{2}\lambda
. Then, choosing the rest frame of either particle 1 or 2, we find

\displaystyle\xi_{-}^{I}

\displaystyle=\xi_{-}^{J}=\left(e^{i\varphi/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2},e^{-i\varphi/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\right),\quad\xi_{+}^{I}=\xi_{+}^{J}=\left(e^{i\varphi/2}\sin\frac{\theta}{2},-e^{-i\varphi/2}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\,,

(42)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{IJ}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(43)

\displaystyle{}_{h}\mathcal{Y}\overbrace{{}^{11\cdots 1}}^{\ell-m}\overbrace{{}^{22\cdots 2}}^{\ell+m}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell+h}N\,[{}_{-h}Y_{\ell,m}(\theta,\varphi)]^{*}\,,

(44)

\displaystyle{}_{h}\mathcal{Y}\underbrace{{}_{11\cdots 1}}_{\ell-m}\underbrace{{}_{22\cdots 2}}_{\ell+m}

\displaystyle=N\,{}_{h}Y_{\ell,m}(\theta,\varphi)\,,

(45)

with
N=\sqrt{\frac{(\ell+m)!(\ell-m)!}{(\ell!)^{2}(2\ell)!}}
.

Appendix B
Thermal radiation from the
S
-matrix

Thermal systems are usually described by mixed states. This may seem at odds with our amplitude-based framework, in which thermal radiation arises from transitions between pure states. In this appendix, we clarify the consistency between the two descriptions by discussing how observables restricted to a subsystem are computed from the
S
-matrix, and then demonstrate in a concrete example how thermality emerges from an amplitude-based computation.

Let us first consider the computation of general observables. We introduce an operator,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)
, acting on a subsystem of the total Hilbert space,
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\beta}
, where
\ket{\alpha}
denotes the visible sector and
\ket{\beta}
denotes the invisible sector:

\bra{\alpha';\beta'}\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)\ket{\alpha;\beta}=\bra{\alpha'}\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)\ket{\alpha}\delta(\beta-\beta^{\prime})\,.

(46)

For an initial pure state
\ket{\Psi}
, the observable at future infinity is computed by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\bra{\Psi}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)\hat{S}\ket{\Psi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\bra{\Psi}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\ket{\alpha';\beta'}\bra{\alpha';\beta'}\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)\ket{\alpha;\beta}\bra{\alpha;\beta}\hat{S}\ket{\Psi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}\bra{\alpha'}\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)\ket{\alpha}P_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}

(47)

where
\int_{\alpha}
denotes the on-shell integral and

\displaystyle P_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha}=\int_{\beta}\bra{\Psi}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\ket{\alpha';\beta}\bra{\alpha;\beta}\hat{S}\ket{\Psi}\,,

(48)

is the product of two
S
-matrix elements summed over all invisible
\beta
states. Its diagonal part
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha
gives the familiar inclusive probabilities. The observable (
47
) can be rewritten compactly as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\alpha)={\rm Tr}_{\alpha}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)\hat{\rho}_{\alpha})\,,

(49)

with

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\int_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}P_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\ket{\alpha'}\bra{\alpha}\,,

(50)

and
{\rm Tr}_{\alpha}(\hat{A})=\int_{\alpha}\bra{\alpha}\hat{A}\ket{\alpha}
. Therefore, the observable can be computed using the reduced density matrix for the visible sector
\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}
. It is computed by tracing out the invisible sector:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}={\rm Tr}_{\beta}(\hat{\rho}_{\alpha\beta})\,,\quad\hat{\rho}_{\alpha\beta}=\hat{S}\ket{\Psi}\bra{\Psi}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\,.

(51)

Although the full final state
\hat{S}\ket{\Psi}
remains pure by unitarity, the reduced states described by
\hat{\rho}_{\alpha}
are mixed whenever the
S
-matrix entangles these two sectors. Thus, there is no conflict in describing any observables confined to the visible sector between an
S
-matrix description in the full Hilbert space and a density-matrix description in the reduced Hilbert space.

Having established this general point, we now show how thermal features emerge in amplitude-based calculations. As a concrete example, consider the number operator of the massless spin-0 particle,
\hat{n}_{k}=\hat{a}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}
. For an initial
state
\ket{\Psi}
, the expectation value of the number of massless particles radiated to infinity is given by

\displaystyle n_{k}=\bra{\Psi}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{n}_{k}\hat{S}\ket{\Psi}\,,

(52)

which can be rewritten in terms of amplitudes by inserting the completeness relations. An important point is that
\hat{n}_{k}
acts only on the massless sector and is therefore blind to the microstate of the massive particle. Thermality emerges precisely from summing over this unresolved internal degeneracy. We thus keep the microstate label
v
explicit in the following, and write the one-particle completeness relation for the massive sector as

\displaystyle\hat{1}=\sum_{s,v}\int\differential\mu^{2}\int\differential\Phi(p)\ket{p,s,v}\bra{p,s,v}\,.

(53)

For simplicity, we take the initial state to be a single-particle state with momentum
p_{2}
and spin-
s_{2}
(strictly speaking, a wave packet state
should be used to describe a localized object, but it does not affect the main disucssion below). We further restrict our analysis to the one-massless particle emission process, which well approximates Hawking radiation as shown in Ref.
Aoki et al. 2025a
. If a system thermalizes after time evolution, the observable
n_{k}
should be independent of the concrete realization of the initial microstate. To keep track of the initial state dependence, we write the initial state as

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi}=\sum_{v_{2}}c_{v_{2}}\ket{p_2,s_2,v_2}\,,\qquad\sum_{v_{2}}|c_{v_{2}}|^{2}=1\,.

(54)

Inserting (
53
), the number of radiated quanta becomes

\displaystyle n_{k}=\sum_{s_{1},v_{1},v_{2},v^{\prime}_{2}}\int\differential\mu^{2}_{1}\int\differential\Phi(p_{1})c_{v_{2}^{\prime}}^{*}c_{v_{2}}\bra{p_2,s_2,v'_2}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\ket{p_1,s_1,v_1;k}\bra{p_1,s_1,v_1;k}\hat{S}\ket{p_2,s_2,v_2}\,.

(55)

We now assume that the microstate couplings
g_{v_{1},v_{2}}
(while suppressing other labels) have (i) equal moduli and (ii) uncorrelated phases, namely

\displaystyle g_{v_{1},v_{2}}=e^{i\theta_{v_{1},v_{2}}}g\,.

(56)

The transition amplitudes between microstates then take the form

\displaystyle\bra{p_1,s_1,v_1;k}\hat{S}\ket{p_2,s_2,v_2}=e^{i\theta_{v_{1},v_{2}}}\bra{p_1,s_1;k}\hat{S}\ket{p_2,s_2}

(57)

where
\ket{p_1,s_1}
and
\ket{p_2,s_2}
denote representative states
with coupling
g
. The microstate labels thus enter only through phases, while the modulus is universal. Under these assumptions, and in the limit of a large number of degeneracies, we find

\displaystyle n_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1},v_{1},v_{2}}\int\differential\mu^{2}_{1}\int\differential\Phi(p_{1})|c_{v_{2}}|^{2}\bra{p_2,s_2}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\ket{p_1,s_1;k}\bra{p_1,s_1;k}\hat{S}\ket{p_2,s_2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1}}\int\differential\mu^{2}_{1}\int\differential\Phi(p_{1})\rho(\mu_{1},s_{1})|\bra{p_1,s_1;k}\hat{S}\ket{p_2,s_2}|^{2}\,,

(58)

where the spectral density
\rho
counts the number of microstates
v_{1}
. As a result, the number of radiated quanta—an expectation value in the pure final state
\hat{S}\ket{\Psi}
—is thus computed from the squared three-point amplitude weighted by the spectral density, independently of the initial state
c_{v}
. Thermal radiation emerges once this inclusive probability takes the form (
22
), as derived in the main text.

Our assumptions of (i) equal moduli and (ii) uncorrelated phases for the microstate couplings are reminiscent of the eigenstate thermalization hypothesis (ETH)
Deutsch 1991
;
Srednicki 1994
;
Deutsch 2018
;
Srednicki 1999
;
Rigol and Srednicki 2012
. Assumption (ii) makes the off-diagonal contributions in (
55
) add up as a random phase so that they are exponentially suppressed by
e^{-\mathcal{S}/2}
relative to the diagonal ones. Assumption (i) further renders the final result independent of the concrete realization of the initial microstates. These two roles parallel the two ETH assumptions described in Ref.
Rigol and Srednicki 2012
, which constrain the matrix elements of a Hermitian operator so as to relate the observables of one eigenstate to an ensemble average. Note, however, that our assumptions concern the
S
-matrix elements rather than those of a Hermitian operator. They are also closely related to the statements about the kinematic structure of partial-wave absorption and emission. As mentioned in the main text, without assumption (ii), there are interference terms between different spin states, which would lead to unwanted angular dependence of the discontinuities. It would be interesting to explore the interplay of kinematics, macroscopic symmetries, and the ETH from the perspective of on-shell amplitudes.

Before closing this section, it is worth mentioning how the above calculations must be modified if the massive states cannot be treated as asymptotic states. First, one can no longer prepare
\ket{p_2,s_2}
as an initial state; instead, one must consider a gravitational collapse from stable asymptotic states, e.g., a super-Planckian collision of elementary particles
Giddings and Srednicki 2008
;
Giddings and Porto 2010
. This is indeed analogous to Hawking’s derivation of thermal radiation
Hawking 1976
, where the BH is formed dynamically by gravitational collapse rather than postulated as an eternal asymptotic object. Second, the BH sector must not be traced out as the intermediate cut states. Recall that the observable
\bra{\Psi}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\alpha)\hat{S}\ket{\Psi}
is interpreted as a weighted on-shell cut of the
\Psi\to\Psi

S
-matrix element. If the BHs are treated as resonances rather than the asymptotic states, their cuts should expose their decay products
Veltman 1963
;
Denner and Lang 2015
, not the BH states themselves. These products may contribute to the visible sector, offering an on-shell approach to one of the central questions of quantum gravity: where information resides after BH evaporation.

Appendix C
The computation of probabilities

In this appendix we provide a detailed computation of the absorption and emission probabilities. Since the probability of emitting a partial wave
\ell
and helicity
h
can be obtained from the probability of absorbing a partial wave
\ell
and helicity
h
with the replacement
1\leftrightarrow 2
,
\omega\to-\omega
and
h\to-h
, we only focus on the absorption probabilities. We start with the
\ell=0,1,2
cases. Then, by building on these explicit cases, we will derive the result for generic values of
\ell
presented in the main text.

C.1
Partial wave probabilities

Our starting point is the absorption probability for generic values of
\ell
in the center-of-mass frame:

\displaystyle P_{\ell,m}^{\rm abs}=\sum_{s_{2}}\int\differential\mu^{2}\rho(\mu^{2})\int\differential\Phi(p_{2})|\bra{p_2,s_2}S\ket{\phi,\gamma}|^{2}\,,

(59)

where the massive particle state and the massless particle state are treated as wavepacket as

\displaystyle\ket{\phi}=\int\differential\Phi(p)\phi(p)\ket{p,\alpha}\,,

(60)

and

\displaystyle\ket{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}\omega\gamma(\omega)\ket{\omega, \ell,m,h}\,,

(61)

\displaystyle\ket{\omega, \ell,m,h}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2\omega}}{4\pi}\int\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}\,{}_{-h}Y_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}})\ket{k,h}\,,

(62)

where
\hat{\bm{k}}=\bm{k}/\omega=(\cos\varphi\sin\theta,\sin\varphi\sin\theta,\cos\theta)
. The normalization of the spherical wave state and the plane wave state are given by

\displaystyle\bra{\omega',\ell',m',h' }\ket{\omega,\ell,m,h}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}\delta_{m^{\prime},m}\delta_{h^{\prime},h}\hat{\delta}(\omega^{\prime}-\omega)\,,

\displaystyle\bra{k',h'}\ket{k,h}

\displaystyle=2\omega\hat{\delta}^{3}(\bm{k}^{\prime}-\bm{k})\delta_{h^{\prime},h}\,,

(63)

and the wavepacket states are normalized to be unity. The wavepacket for the massive state is chosen to be sharply peaked at the momentum of the wavepacket while it is sufficiently broadened to have a well-defined position; as a concrete realization, for example, we can use a wavepacket
Kosower et al. 2019
;
Cristofoli et al. 2022
:

\displaystyle\phi(p_{1})=\mathcal{N}M_{1}^{-2}\exp\left[-\frac{p_{1}\cdot u}{M_{1}\xi}\right],\qquad\frac{\omega^{2}}{M_{1}^{2}}\ll\xi\ll 1\,,

(64)

where
u
is the four-velocity of the wavepacket and
\mathcal{N}
is the normalization factor. This wavepacket satisfies

\displaystyle\phi^{*}(p^{\prime}_{1})\phi(p_{1})=|\phi(\bar{p}_{1})|^{2}\,,\qquad\bar{p}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(p_{1}+p^{\prime}_{1})\,.

(65)

Thus, the wavepacket (
64
) localizes the momentum to be
\bar{p}_{1}\propto u_{1}
, and the absorption probability is

\displaystyle P_{\ell,m}^{\rm abs}

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}\omega\hat{\rm d}\omega^{\prime}\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}\frac{\sqrt{4\omega\omega^{\prime}}}{8\pi}\hat{\delta}(2\bar{p}_{1}\cdot(k-k^{\prime})){}_{-h}Y^{*}_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}){}_{-h}Y_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}})\gamma^{*}(\omega^{\prime})\gamma(\omega)\frac{{\rm Disc}\mathcal{A}_{1^{\prime}\leftarrow 1}^{-h,h}}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}\omega\hat{\rm d}\omega^{\prime}\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}\frac{\sqrt{4\omega\omega^{\prime}}}{8\pi}\hat{\delta}(2M_{2}(\omega-\omega^{\prime})){}_{-h}Y^{*}_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}){}_{-h}Y_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}})\gamma^{*}(\omega^{\prime})\gamma(\omega)\frac{{\rm Disc}\mathcal{A}_{1^{\prime}\leftarrow 1}^{-h,h}}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\omega|}{8\pi M_{2}}\int\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}\,{}_{-h}Y_{\ell,m}^{*}(\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}){}_{-h}Y_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}})~\frac{{\rm Disc}\mathcal{A}_{1^{\prime}\leftarrow 1}^{-h,h}}{2\pi i}\,,

(66)

where

\displaystyle{\rm Disc}\mathcal{A}_{1^{\prime}\leftarrow 1}^{-h,h}\hat{\delta}^{(4)}(p_{1}+k-p_{1}^{\prime}-k^{\prime})=i\sum_{s_{2}}\int\differential\mu^{2}\rho(\mu^{2})\int\differential\Phi(p_{2})\bra{p_1',\alpha;k',h}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\ket{p_2,s_2}\bra{p_2,s_2}\hat{S}\ket{p_1,\alpha;k,h}\,.

(67)

To obtain the second line, we have used
2\bar{p}_{1}\cdot(k-k^{\prime})=2p_{2}\cdot(k-k^{\prime})
for
p_{2}=p_{1}+k=p_{1}^{\prime}+k^{\prime}
and
k^{2}=k^{\prime}{}^{2}=0
, and the fact that the frequencies are defined in the center-of-mass frame. Then, we have assumed the massless wavepacket
\gamma(\omega)
is also localized at a certain frequency, which we rename
\omega
after performing the integral. Finally, assuming uncorrelated phases of microstate couplings, we obtain

\displaystyle P^{\text{abs}}_{\ell,m}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{|\omega|}{8\pi M_{2}}\int\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}\,{}_{-h}Y_{\ell,m}^{*}(\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}){}_{-h}Y_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}})e^{-\|\alpha\|^{2}}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2},\ell^{\prime}}\frac{\rho(M_{2},s_{2})}{(2s_{1})!}||\mathcal{A}^{(\ell^{\prime})}_{s_{1},s_{2},h}||^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\omega|}{8\pi M_{2}}\int\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}\,{}_{-h}Y_{\ell,m}^{*}(\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}){}_{-h}Y_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}})e^{-\|\alpha\|^{2}}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\frac{\rho(M_{2},s_{2})}{(2s_{1})!}||\mathcal{A}^{(\ell)}_{s_{1},s_{2},h}||^{2}

(68)

where the second line follows from the orthogonality of the spin-weighted spherical harmonics. The modulus squared of the
\ell
amplitude reads

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2},h}^{(\ell)}||^{2}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell-\Delta s}|g_{s_{1},s_{2},h}^{(\ell)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s_{1}}\}}{}_{-h}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\{K_{1}\ldots K_{\ell-\Delta s}}{}_{\{J_{1}\ldots J_{\Delta s+\ell}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s_{1}}\}}{}_{J_{2s_{2}}\}}

(69)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times{}_{h}\mathcal{Y}_{\{I_{1}\ldots I_{\ell-\Delta s}}{}^{\{J_{1}\ldots J_{\Delta s+\ell}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s_{1}}\}}{}^{J_{2s_{2}}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s_{1}}\}}\,,

and we introduce the notation
\Delta s=s_{2}-s_{1}
. For convenience, we further define the following tensorial objects

\displaystyle{}_{h}\mathcal{Y}_{I_{1}\cdots I_{\ell-\Delta s}}{}^{J_{1}\cdots J_{\ell+\Delta s}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\epsilon_{I_{1}K_{1}}\cdots\epsilon_{I_{\ell-\Delta s}K_{\ell-\Delta s}}\,{}_{h}\mathcal{Y}^{K_{1}\cdots K_{\ell-\Delta s}J_{1}\cdots J_{\ell+\Delta s}}~\,,

(70)

\displaystyle{}_{h}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{I_{1}I_{2}\cdots I_{2\ell}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(-1)^{\ell+h}\,{}_{h}\mathcal{Y}_{I_{1}I_{2}\cdots I_{2\ell}}=\left({}_{-h}\mathcal{Y}^{I_{1}I_{2}\cdots I_{2\ell}}\right)^{*}~\,,

(71)

\displaystyle{}_{h}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{I_{1}\cdots I_{\ell-\Delta s}}{}_{J_{1}\cdots J_{\ell+\Delta s}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\epsilon^{I_{1}K_{1}}\cdots\epsilon^{I_{\ell-\Delta s}K_{\ell-\Delta s}}\,{}_{h}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{K_{1}\cdots K_{\ell-\Delta s}J_{1}\cdots J_{\ell+\Delta s}}=(-1)^{\ell-\Delta s}\left({}_{-h}\mathcal{Y}_{I_{1}\cdots I_{\ell-\Delta s}}{}^{J_{1}\cdots J_{\ell+\Delta s}}\right)^{*}\,,

(72)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{IJ}:=-\mathcal{E}_{IJ}\;,

(73)

so that
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{I}{}_{J}=(\mathcal{E}_{I}{}^{J})^{*}
, where the equalities relating complex conjugates hold for real kinematics. Throughout our discussion, we will make use of the so-called Schoonschip notation
Strubbe 1974
;
Veltman and Williams 1991

\displaystyle{}_{h}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha^{n}}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{m}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tilde{\alpha}_{K_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\alpha}_{K_{m}}\ {}_{h}\mathcal{Y}_{I_{1}\ldots I_{n}}{}^{K_{1}\ldots K_{m}}\alpha^{I_{1}}\ldots\alpha^{I_{n}}\;,

(74)

\displaystyle{}_{h}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{m}}{}_{\alpha^{n}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tilde{\alpha}_{K_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\alpha}_{K_{m}}\ {}_{h}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{K_{1}\ldots K_{m}}{}_{I_{1}\ldots I_{n}}\alpha^{I_{1}}\ldots\alpha^{I_{n}}\;,

(75)

such that in the presence of free indices we have

\displaystyle{}_{h}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha^{n}}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{m-r}J_{1}\ldots J_{r}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tilde{\alpha}_{K_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\alpha}_{K_{m-r}}\ {}_{h}\mathcal{Y}_{I_{1}\ldots I_{n}}{}^{K_{1}\ldots K_{m-r}J_{1}\ldots J_{r}}\alpha^{I_{1}}\ldots\alpha^{I_{n}}\;,

(76)

\displaystyle{}_{h}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{m}}{}_{\alpha^{n-r}J_{1}\ldots J_{r}}{}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\tilde{\alpha}_{K_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\alpha}_{K_{m}}\ {}_{h}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{K_{1}\ldots K_{m}}{}_{I_{1}\ldots I_{n-r}J_{1}\ldots J_{r}}\alpha^{I_{1}}\ldots\alpha^{I_{n-r}}\;.

(77)

Henceforth, we drop the
h
subscript in order to reduce clutter, but it should be understood that the results are valid for arbitrary values of
h
.

C.2
The
\ell=0
case

Before turning to the
\ell>0
case, let us briefly comment on
\ell=0
. For this value, the only allowed transition is
s_{1}=s\to s_{2}=s
, and the corresponding amplitude admits a unique structure

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s,s}^{(\ell=0)}||^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{|g_{s,s}^{(\ell=0)}|^{2}}{4\pi}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s_{1}}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\{K_{1}}{}_{\{J_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s_{1}}\}}{}_{J_{2s_{2}}\}}\times\mathcal{E}{}_{\{J_{1}}{}^{\{I_{1}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s_{1}}\}}{}^{J_{2s_{2}}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s_{1}}\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{|g_{s,s}^{(\ell=0)}|^{2}}{4\pi}\|\alpha\|^{4s}\,,

(78)

where the factor of
1/4\pi
has been included to match the normalization used for the
\ell\neq 0
case. Hence, the modulus squared exhibits the desired kinematic structure, and spin universality does not arise from kinematics alone. Instead, spin universality is required to cancel the normalization factor
e^{-\|\alpha\|^{2}}
of the spin coherent state:

\displaystyle|g_{s,s}^{(\ell=0)}|^{2}=|g^{(\ell=0)}|^{2}~~\text{for all }s\implies e^{-\|\alpha\|^{2}}\sum_{s}\frac{\rho(M_{2})}{(2s)!}||\mathcal{A}_{s,s}^{(\ell=0)}||^{2}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\rho(M_{2})|g^{(\ell=0)}|^{2}\,.

(79)

On the other hand, spin universality arises from purely kinematic reasons for
\ell\neq 0
which is the focus of the remainder of our discussion.

C.3
The
\ell=1
case

For
\ell=1
the modulus squared of the amplitude takes the form

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=1)}||^{2}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1-\Delta s}|g_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s_{1}}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\{K_{1}\ldots K_{1-\Delta s}}{}_{\{J_{1}\ldots J_{\Delta s+1}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s_{1}}\}}{}_{J_{2s_{2}}\}}

(80)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\mathcal{Y}_{\{I_{1}\ldots I_{1-\Delta s}}{}^{\{J_{1}\ldots J_{\Delta s+1}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s_{1}}\}}{}^{J_{2s_{2}}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s_{1}}\}}\,.

As discussed in the main text, the possible transitions for this value of angular momentum are
\Delta s=-1,0,1
. In other words, the only allowed absorption processes (and thus emission by CPT) are
s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s-1
,
s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s
, and
s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s+1
. Since each process leads to a different structure of (
80
), we will consider each one in turn.

The first process is
s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s-1
for which (
80
) simply evaluates to

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=1)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=-1}

\displaystyle=|g_{s,s-1}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\{K_{1}K_{2}}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{3}}{}_{\{J_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s}\}}{}_{J_{2s-2}\}}\mathcal{Y}_{\{I_{1}I_{2}}\mathcal{E}_{I_{3}}{}^{\{J_{1}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s}\}}{}^{J_{2s-2}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s}\}}

(81)

\displaystyle=|g_{s,s-1}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-4}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha}\,.

The second process is
s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s
and yields

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=1)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=0}

\displaystyle=-|g_{s,s}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\{K_{1}}{}_{\{J_{1}}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2}}{}_{J_{2}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s}\}}{}_{J_{2s}\}}\mathcal{Y}_{\{I_{1}}{}^{\{J_{1}}\mathcal{E}_{I_{2}}{}^{J_{2}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s}\}}{}^{J_{2s}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s}\}}

(82)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|g_{s,s}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-4}}{2s}\left(||\alpha||^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{J}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}{}^{J}+(2s-1)\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}}\right)\,,

where the numerical prefactors are purely combinatorial. The final process is
s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s+1
and requires a little more care. In this case, (
80
) reads

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=1)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=1}

\displaystyle=|g_{s,s+1}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\{J_{1}J_{2}}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\{K_{1}}{}_{J_{3}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s}\}}{}_{J_{2s+2}\}}\mathcal{Y}^{\{J_{1}J_{2}}\mathcal{E}_{\{I_{1}}{}^{J_{3}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s}\}}{}^{J_{2s+2}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s}\}}

(83)

\displaystyle=|g_{s,s+1}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{J_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\alpha}_{J_{2s}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{J_{2s+1}J_{2s+2}\}}\alpha^{\{J_{1}}\ldots\alpha^{J_{2s}}\mathcal{Y}^{J_{2s+1}J_{2s+2}\}}\,.

To actually go ahead and evaluate this, we will need to think a little deeper about combinatorics. The symmetrized products are expanded as

\alpha^{\{J_{1}}\ldots\alpha^{J_{2s}}\mathcal{Y}^{J_{2s+1}J_{2s+2}\}}=\frac{2}{(2s+2)(2s+1)}\left(\alpha^{J_{1}}\ldots\alpha^{J_{2s}}\mathcal{Y}^{J_{2s+1}J_{2s+2}}+\text{permutations}\right)\,,

(84)

where the prefactor comes from averaging over the possible positions of the two indices of
\mathcal{Y}
. If we fix the positions of the indices on
\mathcal{Y}
, then there are three distinct cases for how the indices of
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
can be placed with respect to them. The first case is when the indices of
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
never contract with
\mathcal{Y}
, which yields

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{\{J_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\alpha}_{J_{2s}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{J_{2s+1}J_{2s+2}\}}\alpha^{\{J_{1}}\ldots\alpha^{J_{2s}}\mathcal{Y}^{J_{2s+1}J_{2s+2}\}}\supset\frac{2s(2s-1)}{2}||\alpha||^{4s-4}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha\alpha}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\,.

(85)

The second case is when only one index of
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
contracts with
\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{\{J_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\alpha}_{J_{2s}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{J_{2s+1}J_{2s+2}\}}\alpha^{\{J_{1}}\ldots\alpha^{J_{2s}}\mathcal{Y}^{J_{2s+1}J_{2s+2}\}}\supset 2(2s)||\alpha||^{4s-2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha J}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}J}\,.

(86)

Finally, the third case is when both indices of
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
contract with
\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{\{J_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\alpha}_{J_{2s}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{J_{2s+1}J_{2s+2}\}}\alpha^{\{J_{1}}\ldots\alpha^{J_{2s}}\mathcal{Y}^{J_{2s+1}J_{2s+2}\}}\supset||\alpha||^{4s}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{J_{1}J_{2}}\mathcal{Y}^{J_{1}J_{2}}\,.

(87)

Putting all of these cases together, we find

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=1)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=1}

\displaystyle=\frac{|g_{s,s+1}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-4}}{(2s+2)(2s+1)}\Big(2||\alpha||^{4}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{J_{1}J_{2}}\mathcal{Y}^{J_{1}J_{2}}\ +4(2s)||\alpha||^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha J}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}J}+2s(2s-1)\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha\alpha}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\Big)\,.

As we are interested in a classical slow-rotating BH, for which we have
s\gg 1
, the results are simplified to

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=1)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=0}\simeq-|g_{s,s}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-4}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}}\,,\quad||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=1)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=1}\simeq|g_{s,s+1}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-4}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha\alpha}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\,.

(88)

Finally, summing over all transition processes yields the transition-inclusive modulus squared

||\mathcal{A}_{s}^{(\ell=1)}||^{2}=||\alpha||^{4s-4}\Big(|g_{s,s-1}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha}-|g_{s,s}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}}+|g_{s,s+1}^{(\ell=1)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha\alpha}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\Big)\,.

(89)

C.4
The
\ell=2
case

In the case of
\ell=2
, the modulus squared of the amplitude takes the form

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=2)}||^{2}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{2-\Delta s}|g_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s_{1}}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\{K_{1}\ldots K_{2-\Delta s}}{}_{\{J_{1}\ldots J_{\Delta s+2}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s_{1}}\}}{}_{J_{2s_{2}}\}}

(90)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\mathcal{Y}_{\{I_{1}\ldots I_{2-\Delta s}}{}^{\{J_{1}\ldots J_{\Delta s+2}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s_{1}}\}}{}^{J_{2s_{2}}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s_{1}}\}}\,,

for which there are five possible transitions
-2\leq\Delta s\leq 2
. For each given transition, the amplitudes take the form

\displaystyle s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s-2:

\displaystyle~\mathcal{Y}_{\left\{I_{1}I_{2}I_{3}I_{4}\right.}\mathcal{E}_{I_{5}}{}^{\left\{J_{1}\right.}\cdots\mathcal{E}_{\left.I_{2s}\right\}}{{}^{\left.J_{2s-4}\right\}}}\;,

\displaystyle s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s-1:

\displaystyle~\mathcal{Y}_{\left\{I_{1}I_{2}I_{3}\right.}{}^{\left\{J_{1}\right.}\mathcal{E}_{I_{4}}{}^{J_{2}}\cdots\mathcal{E}_{\left.I_{2s}\right\}}{{}^{\left.J_{2s-2}\right\}}}\;,

\displaystyle s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s:

\displaystyle~\mathcal{Y}_{\left\{I_{1}I_{2}\right.}{}^{\left\{J_{1}J_{2}\right.}\mathcal{E}_{I_{3}}{}^{J_{3}}\cdots\mathcal{E}_{\left.I_{2s}\right\}}{{}^{\left.J_{2s}\right\}}}\;,

\displaystyle s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s+1:

\displaystyle~\mathcal{Y}_{\left\{I_{1}\right.}{}^{\left\{J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}\right.}\mathcal{E}_{I_{2}}{}^{J_{4}}\cdots\mathcal{E}_{\left.I_{2s}\right\}}{{}^{\left.J_{2s+2}\right\}}}\;,

\displaystyle s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s+2:

\displaystyle~\mathcal{Y}^{\left\{J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}J_{4}\right.}\mathcal{E}_{\left\{I_{1}\right.}{}^{J_{5}}\cdots\mathcal{E}_{\left.I_{2s}\right\}}{{}^{\left.J_{2s+4}\right\}}}\;.

As before, we simply need to compute each case separately, keeping in mind that the combinatorial factors follow straightforwardly from our previous discussion. To this end, each transition yields

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=2)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=-2}

\displaystyle=|g_{s,s-2}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\{K_{1}K_{2}K_{3}K_{4}}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{5}}{}_{\{J_{1}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s}\}}{}_{J_{2s-4}\}}\mathcal{Y}_{\{I_{1}I_{2}I_{3}I_{4}}\mathcal{E}_{I_{5}}{}^{\{J_{1}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s}\}}{}^{J_{2s-4}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s}\}}

(91)

\displaystyle=|g_{s,s-2}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-8}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha\alpha\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=2)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=-1}

\displaystyle=-|g_{s,s-1}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\{K_{1}K_{2}K_{3}}{}_{\{J_{1}}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{4}}{}_{J_{2}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s}\}}{}_{J_{2s-2}\}}\mathcal{Y}_{\{I_{1}I_{2}I_{3}}{}^{\{J_{1}}\mathcal{E}_{I_{4}}{}^{J_{2}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s}\}}{}^{J_{2s-2}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s}\}}

(92)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|g_{s,s-1}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-8}}{2s-2}\Big(||\alpha||^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{J}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha\alpha}{}^{J}+(2s-3)\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}}\Big)\,,

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=2)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=0}

\displaystyle=|g_{s,s}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\{K_{1}K_{2}}{}_{\{J_{1}J_{2}}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{3}}{}_{J_{3}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s}\}}{}_{J_{2s}\}}\mathcal{Y}_{\{I_{1}I_{2}}{}^{\{J_{1}J_{2}}\mathcal{E}_{I_{3}}{}^{J_{3}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s}\}}{}^{J_{2s}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s}\}}

(93)

\displaystyle=\frac{|g_{s,s}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-8}}{2s(2s-1)}\Big(2||\alpha||^{4}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{J_{1}J_{2}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha}{}^{J_{1}J_{2}}+4(2s-2)||\alpha||^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha J}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}J}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+(2s-2)(2s-3)\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha\alpha}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\Big)\,,

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=2)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=1}

\displaystyle=-|g_{s,s+1}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\{K_{1}}{}_{\{J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2}}{}_{J_{4}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s}\}}{}_{J_{2s+2}\}}\mathcal{Y}_{\{I_{1}}{}^{\{J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}}\mathcal{E}_{I_{2}}{}^{J_{4}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s}\}}{}^{J_{2s+2}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s}\}}

(94)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|g_{s,s+1}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-8}}{2s(2s+1)(2s+2)}\Big(6||\alpha||^{6}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}{}^{J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}}+18(2s-1)||\alpha||^{4}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha J_{1}J_{2}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}J_{1}J_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+9(2s-1)(2s-2)||\alpha||^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha\alpha J}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}J}+(2s-1)(2s-2)(2s-3)\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha\alpha\alpha}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\Big)\,,

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell=2)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s=2}

\displaystyle=|g_{s,s+2}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{\{K_{2s}\}}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\{J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}J_{4}}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\{K_{1}}{}_{J_{5}}\ldots\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{K_{2s}\}}{}_{J_{2s+4}\}}\mathcal{Y}^{\{J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}J_{4}}\mathcal{E}_{\{I_{1}}{}^{J_{5}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s}\}}{}^{J_{2s+4}\}}\alpha^{\{I_{2s}\}}

(95)

\displaystyle=\frac{|g_{s,s+2}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-8}}{(2s+1)(2s+2)(2s+3)(2s+4)}\Big(24||\alpha||^{8}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}J_{4}}\mathcal{Y}^{J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}J_{4}}+96(2s)||\alpha||^{6}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}J_{1}J_{2}J_{3}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+72(2s)(2s-1)||\alpha||^{4}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha\alpha J_{1}J_{2}}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}J_{1}J_{2}}+16(2s)(2s-1)(2s-2)||\alpha||^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha\alpha\alpha J}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}J}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+(2s)(2s-1)(2s-2)(2s-3)\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha\alpha\alpha\alpha}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\Big)\,.

Then, for a classical slow-rotating BH, only the leading structures proportional to
||\alpha||^{4s-8}
contribute, such that

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s}^{(\ell=2)}||^{2}=||\alpha||^{4s-8}\Big(

\displaystyle|g_{s,s-2}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha\alpha\alpha}-|g_{s,s-1}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}}+|g_{s,s}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha\alpha}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}

(96)

\displaystyle-|g_{s,s+1}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}}{}_{\alpha\alpha\alpha}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}+|g_{s,s+2}^{(\ell=2)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\alpha\alpha\alpha\alpha}\mathcal{Y}^{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\alpha}}\Big)\,.

C.5
The generic
\ell
case

Given our analysis for the
\ell=1
and
\ell=2
cases, it is straightforward to extend the result to generic values of
\ell
by identifying the underlying pattern. Towards this end, we take
s_{1}=s
and
s_{2}=s+\Delta s
with the possible transitions controlled by
-\ell\leq\Delta s\leq\ell
. Note that for a given value of
\Delta s
, the amplitude schematically takes the form

\mathcal{Y}_{\{I_{1}\ldots I_{p}}{}^{\{J_{1}\ldots J_{q}}\mathcal{E}_{I_{p+1}}{}^{J_{q+1}}\ldots\mathcal{E}_{I_{2s}\}}{}^{J_{2s+2\Delta s}\}}\;,

(97)

where
p=\ell-\Delta s
and
q=\ell+\Delta s
. Note that, in our convention for
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
, rewriting the complex conjugate of a mixed-index tensor with
p
lowered indices in terms of
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
produces a factor of
(-1)^{p}=(-1)^{\ell-\Delta s}
. Once we have contracted everything with
\tilde{\alpha}
and
\alpha
when computing the modulus squared of the amplitude, the remaining combinatorial factors depend on how the
q

J
-indices of
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
overlap with the
q

J
-indices of
\mathcal{Y}
. One can then conclude that for a given
\Delta s
the modulus squared of the amplitude takes the form

||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s}=(-1)^{p}|g_{s,s+\Delta s}^{(\ell)}|^{2}\sum_{r=0}^{q}\frac{\binom{q}{r}\binom{n_{s}-q}{q-r}}{\binom{n_{s}}{q}}||\alpha||^{2n_{s}-4q+2r}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\,p}}{}_{\alpha^{\,q-r}J_{1}\ldots J_{r}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha^{\,p}}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\,q-r}J_{1}\ldots J_{r}}\,,

(98)

with
n_{s}=2s+2\Delta s
, and where
r
is the number of overlapping indices. Because we are ultimately interested in a classical slow-rotating object, we then take the limit
s\gg 1
. In this limit, we have that the combinatorial prefactors behave as
\mathcal{O}(s^{-r})
so that the
r=0
term always dominates. Indeed, noticing that
n_{s}\sim 2s
and
q\sim\ell
, the
r=0
prefactor becomes unity. Thus, for each transition we have

||\mathcal{A}_{s_{1},s_{2}}^{(\ell)}||^{2}\Big\rvert_{\Delta s}\simeq(-1)^{\ell-\Delta s}|g_{s,s+\Delta s}^{(\ell)}|^{2}||\alpha||^{4s-4\ell}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\,\ell-\Delta s}}{}_{\alpha^{\,\ell+\Delta s}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha^{\,\ell-\Delta s}}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\,\ell+\Delta s}}\,,

(99)

up to corrections suppressed by
1/s
. Finally, summing over all transitions we obtain

||\mathcal{A}_{s}^{(\ell)}||^{2}=||\alpha||^{4s-4\ell}\sum_{\Delta s=-\ell}^{\ell}(-1)^{\ell-\Delta s}|g_{s,s+\Delta s}^{(\ell)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\,\ell-\Delta s}}{}_{\alpha^{\,\ell+\Delta s}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha^{\,\ell-\Delta s}}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\,\ell+\Delta s}}\,,

(100)

and thus the absorption probability for generic
\ell
takes the form

\displaystyle P^{\rm abs}_{\ell,m}\simeq\frac{\omega}{8\pi M_{2}}\int

\displaystyle\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}\,Y_{\ell,m}^{*}(\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime})Y_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}})

\displaystyle\times\sum_{s}\frac{\rho(M_{2})}{(2s)!}e^{-||\alpha||^{2}}||\alpha||^{4s-4\ell}\sum_{\Delta s=-\ell}^{\ell}(-1)^{\ell-\Delta s}|g_{s,s+\Delta s}^{(\ell)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\,\ell-\Delta s}}{}_{\alpha^{\,\ell+\Delta s}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha^{\,\ell-\Delta s}}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\,\ell+\Delta s}}\,.

(101)

Appendix D
The emergence of spin universality

In this appendix we provide a discussion of the emergence of spin universality for generic
\ell
values. For consistency with macroscopic spherical symmetry, the probability that a partial wave of angular momentum
\ell
is absorbed must be independent of the orientation of the spin vector. This kinematic consistency implies the probability to be proportional to the
\ell
-th spinning Legendre polynomial
\mathcal{P}^{(\ell)}
, as it is only the object without the directional dependence of
\alpha_{I}
. As we have discussed in the main text, the consequence of this condition is that the modulus squared of each transition coupling for all spinning states is proportional to a single universal coupling, say,
|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}
, and then the probability becomes independent of the size of the classical spin
\|\alpha\|^{2}
as well, correctly describing the
\mathcal{O}(S^{0})
dynamics. Our goal in this appendix is therefore to find the precise relation for all couplings.

Focusing on the approximation in which
s\gg 1
, we have from Appendix
C
that

||\mathcal{A}_{s}^{(\ell)}||^{2}=||\alpha||^{4s-4\ell}\sum_{q=0}^{n}G_{q}^{(\ell)}S_{q}^{(\ell)}\,,

(102)

where
q=\ell+\Delta s
,
n=2\ell
,
G_{q}^{(\ell)}=|g_{s,s-\ell+q}^{(\ell)}|^{2}
, and

S_{q}^{(\ell)}=(-1)^{q}\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\,n-q}}{}_{\alpha^{\,q}}\mathcal{Y}_{\alpha^{\,n-q}}{}^{\tilde{\alpha}^{\,q}}\,,

(103)

because
(-1)^{\ell-\Delta s}=(-1)^{n-q}=(-1)^{q}
. The desired
\mathcal{P}^{(\ell)}
-type angular dependence is obtained by inserting the spinor completeness relation

\delta^{I}{}_{J}=\frac{1}{||\alpha||^{2}}(\alpha^{I}\tilde{\alpha}_{J}-\tilde{\alpha}^{I}\alpha_{J})\,,

(104)

2\ell
times into
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{J_{1}\ldots J_{2\ell}}\mathcal{Y}_{J_{1}\ldots J_{2\ell}}
. This gives

\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\ell)}\coloneqq\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}\mathcal{P}^{(\ell)}=\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{J_{1}\ldots J_{2\ell}}\mathcal{Y}_{J_{1}\ldots J_{2\ell}}=\frac{1}{||\alpha||^{4\ell}}\sum_{q=0}^{n}\binom{n}{q}S_{q}^{(\ell)}\,,

(105)

where we define a normalized spinning Legendre polynomial for convenience, and the binomial vector

\left(\binom{n}{0},\binom{n}{1},\ldots\binom{n}{n}\right)\,,

(106)

is the coefficient vector corresponding to
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\ell)}
. Our next step is to express (
102
) in terms of a basis that contains
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\ell)}
plus some unwanted structures whose coefficients we will ultimately enforce to be zero. Indeed, doing this explicitly for generic values of
\ell
is an involved process, so we should carefully choose a basis for this purpose. It turns out that the so-called Kravchuk polynomials (see, e.g., Ref.
Koekoek et al. 2010
) provide an exceptionally simple way of doing this. The Kravchuk polynomials
K_{m}(q)
for
m=0,\ldots,n
are
defined by

\sum_{m=0}^{n}K_{m}(q)t^{m}=(1-t)^{q}(1+t)^{n-q}\,,

(107)

where
t
is simply an auxiliary bookkeeping variable, whereby the coefficient of each power of
t^{m}
defines
K_{m}(q)
. The Kravchuk polynomials obey the orthogonality relation

\sum_{q=0}^{n}\binom{n}{q}K_{m}(q)K_{m^{\prime}}(q)=2^{n}\binom{n}{m}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\,.

(108)

Thus, this makes them a natural basis for decomposing coefficient vectors with binomial weighting.

Given these polynomials, we define a basis given by

U_{m}^{(\ell)}\coloneqq\frac{1}{||\alpha||^{4\ell}}\sum_{q=0}^{n}\binom{n}{q}K_{m}(k)S_{q}^{(\ell)}\,,

(109)

where, since
K_{0}(q)=1
, the first element is precisely
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\ell)}

U_{0}^{(\ell)}=\frac{1}{||\alpha||^{4\ell}}\sum_{q=0}^{n}\binom{n}{q}S_{q}^{(\ell)}=\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\ell)}\,,

(110)

while the remaining
U_{m}^{(\ell)}
with
m\geq 1
are all unwanted angular structures, dependent on the direction of
\alpha_{I}
. By decomposing the modulus squared of the amplitude summed over all transition processes into this basis

||\mathcal{A}_{s}^{(\ell)}||^{2}=||\alpha||^{4s-4\ell}\sum_{q=0}^{n}G_{q}^{(\ell)}S_{q}^{(\ell)}=||\alpha||^{4s}\sum_{m=0}^{n}c_{m}U_{m}^{(\ell)}\,,

(111)

followed by using the orthogonality of the Kravchuk polynomials, we find

c_{m}=\frac{1}{2^{n}\binom{n}{m}}\sum_{q=0}^{n}G_{q}^{(\ell)}K_{m}(q)\,.

(112)

Specifically, for pure
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\ell)}
dependence we require that
c_{m}=0
for
m\geq 1
, or, equivalently,

\sum_{q=0}^{n}G_{q}^{(\ell)}K_{m}(q)=0\,,\quad m\geq 1\,.

(113)

Imposing this at the level of (
111
)

\displaystyle||\mathcal{A}_{s}^{(\ell)}||^{2}=||\alpha||^{4s-4\ell}\sum_{q=0}^{n}G_{q}^{(\ell)}S_{q}^{(\ell)}=||\alpha||^{4s}c_{0}U_{0}^{(\ell)}=||\alpha||^{4s-4\ell}\sum_{q=0}^{n}c_{0}\binom{n}{q}S_{q}^{(\ell)}\;,

(114)

allows us to read off that

G_{k}^{(\ell)}=c_{0}\binom{n}{q}=|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}\binom{n}{q}\,,

(115)

where the coupling of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\ell)}
is chosen to be
c_{0}=|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}
, such that each modulus squared of the coupling is given by

|g_{s,s+\Delta s}^{(\ell)}|^{2}=|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}\binom{2\ell}{\ell+\Delta s}\,.

(116)

Thus, the modulus of the amplitude squared summed over all transition processes becomes

||\mathcal{A}_{s}^{(\ell)}||^{2}=||\alpha||^{4s-4\ell}\sum_{q=0}^{n}G_{q}^{(\ell)}S_{q}^{(\ell)}=||\alpha||^{4s-4\ell}\sum_{q=0}^{n}|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}\binom{n}{q}S_{q}^{(\ell)}=||\alpha||^{4s}|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\ell)}\,.

(117)

Therefore, the Kravchuk polynomials diagonalize the space of coefficient vectors with binomial weighting. In this basis,
U_{0}^{(\ell)}=\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\ell)}
, while the
U_{m}^{(\ell)}
with
m\geq 1
correspond to unwanted angular structures.

The inclusive emission probability—the probability of emitting a partial wave
\ell
from particle 2, summing over all final states of particle 1—is computed by replacing
1\leftrightarrow 2
and
\omega\to-\omega
. Hence, the consistency with the macroscopic spherical symmetry of emission concludes

\displaystyle|g_{s-\Delta s,s}^{(\ell)}|^{2}=|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}\binom{2\ell}{\ell-\Delta s}\,.

(118)

Here, an identity of the binomial coefficients

\displaystyle\binom{2\ell}{\ell+\Delta s}=\binom{2\ell}{\ell-\Delta s}

(119)

implies that the coupling squares for the spin change
\Delta s
and
-\Delta s
are identical. Note that the absorption and emission processes are not reverse processes.

Figure 1:
Spin transition processes for absorption (left to right) and emission (right to left).

As shown in Fig.
1
, an absorption process corresponds to a process from a fixed
s_{1}
state into all possible
s_{2}
states, and emission is from a fixed
s_{2}
to all
s_{1}
. Macroscopic symmetry has concluded that the
2\ell+1
couplings for a fixed
s_{1}
or
s_{2}
are controlled by the same coupling. However, as clearly seen in Fig.
1
, the absorption and emission processes can share the same spin transition, for instance,
s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s+1
and
s_{1}=s\leftrightarrow s_{2}=s-1
. Therefore, the microscopic CPT invariance, implemented in crossing symmetry of amplitudes, connects the couplings in (
116
) and (
118
), concluding that the couplings of
all
spin transition processes are controlled by a single universal coupling. In short, spin universality emerges from reconciling the microscopic crossing symmetry and the macroscopic symmetry. This is particularly interesting for photons and gravitons, where they have no exceptional
\ell=0
process.

Having obtained spin universality, the absorption probability takes the final form

\displaystyle P^{\rm abs}_{\ell,m}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{|\omega|}{8\pi M_{2}}\int\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}\differential^{2}\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime}\,Y_{\ell,m}^{*}(\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime})Y_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}})\sum_{s}\frac{\rho(M_{2})}{(2s)!}e^{-||\alpha||^{2}}||\alpha||^{4s}|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(\ell)}(\hat{\bm{k}}^{\prime},\hat{\bm{k}})

\displaystyle=\frac{|\omega|}{8\pi M_{2}}\sum_{s}\frac{\rho(M_{2})}{(2s)!}e^{-||\alpha||^{2}}||\alpha||^{4s}|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\omega|}{8\pi M_{2}}\rho(M_{2})|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}\,,

(120)

and the emission probability is

\displaystyle P^{\rm em}_{\ell,m}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\omega|}{8\pi M_{1}}\rho(M_{1})|g^{(\ell)}|^{2}\,,

(121)

where the remaining asymmetry between the two comes from the difference in the phase space volume of the final states.

Appendix E
Greybody factors and relation with emission and absorption probabilities

One of the key equations in our derivation of the thermal spectrum was the use of (
20
), connecting the classical transmissivity, also known as the greybody factor, with the quantum absorption and emission probabilities. In this section, we provide a derivation of this equation by studying a classical wave scattering based on quantum scattering amplitudes. Note that the same equation is discussed in Ref.
Endlich and Penco 2017
under the assumption of weak coupling. The purpose of this section is to obtain (
20
) without using an expansion of couplings because we are interested in objects with strong absorption.

We start by considering an isolated object described as in Ref.
Cristofoli et al. 2022
by a localized massive wavepacket and by an incoming wave made, for simplicity, of massless scalars

\ket{\Psi;\beta}:=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\|\beta\|^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int d\Phi(p,k_{1},...k_{n})\beta(k_{1})...\beta(k_{n})\phi(p)\ket{p,\alpha;k_1,...,k_n}

(122)

with

\displaystyle\|\beta\|^{2}:=\int d\Phi(k)|\beta(k)|^{2}\,.

(123)

Here,
\beta(k)
is a coherent wave shape of the incoming wave, whose magnitude can be read by computing the expectation value of the number operator

\displaystyle\bra{\Psi;\beta}\hat{N}\ket{\Psi;\beta}=\|\beta\|^{2}\,,\qquad\hat{N}:=\int\differential\Phi(k)\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{k}\hat{a}_{k}\,.

(124)

Hence, the intensity change of the coherent wave after scattering is given by

\displaystyle\bra{\Psi;\beta}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{N}\hat{S}\ket{\Psi;\beta}-{\bra{\Psi;\beta}\hat{N}\ket{\Psi;\beta}}=-\int\differential\Phi(k)|\mathcal{T}(k)|^{2}|\beta(k)|^{2}\,,

(125)

where
|\mathcal{T}(k)|>0
represents a classical absorption rate of the coherent wave at the momentum
k
. Using unitarity, the left-hand side can be expressed as

\displaystyle\bra{\Psi;\beta}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{N}\hat{S}\ket{\Psi;\beta}-\bra{\Psi;\beta}\hat{N}\ket{\Psi;\beta}=\bra{\Psi;\beta}\hat{S}^{\dagger}[\hat{N},\hat{S}]\ket{\Psi;\beta}

(126)

which we would like to compute in the following.

We begin by considering the commutator
[\hat{N},\hat{S}]
sandwiched by
n
-particle states:

\displaystyle\bra{p',s';k'_1;\cdots; k_m'}[\hat{N},\hat{S}]\ket{p,s;k_1;\cdots;k_n}=(m-n)\bra{p',s';k'_1;\cdots; k_m'}\hat{S}\ket{p,s;k_1;\cdots;k_n}

(127)

which manifests that only the number change processes contribute to this quantity. Similarly, using the completeness relation, we find

\displaystyle\Delta N_{n^{\prime},n}

\displaystyle=\bra{p',s';k'_1;\cdots; k'_{n'}}\hat{S}^{\dagger}[\hat{N},\hat{S}]\ket{p,s;k_1;\cdots;k_n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}}\int\differential\mu^{2}\int\differential\Phi(p^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}_{1},\cdots,k^{\prime\prime}_{m})\rho(\mu,s^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\bra{p',s';k'_1;\cdots; k'_{n'}}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\ket{p'',s'';k''_1;\cdots;k''_m}\bra{p'',s'';k''_1;\cdots;k''_m}[\hat{N},\hat{S}]\ket{p,s;k_1;\cdots; k_n}

\displaystyle=i\hat{\delta}^{(4)}(P_{n}-P^{\prime}_{n})\delta_{n^{\prime},n}\left(-\sum\hbox to103.32pt{\vbox to37.59pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.92914pt\lower-20.11682pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-18.73848pt}{-19.2246pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 -23.62 M 35.43 -23.62 C 35.43 -17.1 30.15 -11.81 23.62 -11.81 C 17.1 -11.81 11.81 -17.1 11.81 -23.62 C 11.81 -30.15 17.1 -35.43 23.62 -35.43 C 30.15 -35.43 35.43 -30.15 35.43 -23.62 Z M 23.62 -23.62}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{13.18292pt}{-19.57182pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

\displaystyle+\cdots\,,

(128)

where
P_{n}=p+\sum^{n}_{i=1}k
,
P^{\prime}=p^{\prime}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}k^{\prime}
, and the ellipsis stands for contributions from higher-point amplitudes. Here, we use the similar diagrammatic notation used in Chapter 4 of Ref.
Eden et al. 1966
: the
\pm
bubbles denote the
(-1)\times(\text{scattering amplitudes})
and their conjugate, all lines represent on-shell particles with the solid and wavy lines being massive and massless particles, the bold line is the mass-changed state, and the summation is taken over all possible choices of different particles with the same topology of the diagram. The first term describes absorptions; the second term can be understood as induced emissions; and the third one is spontaneous emissions. Crucially, the first two terms have
n\times n!
different pairings while the third one has only
n!
pairings. Hence, the third term can be negligible in the large
n
limit, corresponding to the coherent wave with a classical intensity. As a result, we have

\displaystyle\Delta N_{n^{\prime},n}

\displaystyle\simeq\hat{\delta}^{(4)}(P_{n}-P_{n}^{\prime})\delta_{n^{\prime},n}\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}}\int\differential^{2}\mu\int\differential\Phi(p^{\prime\prime})\rho(\mu,s^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\times\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-\mathcal{A}^{*}_{p^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow p^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}}\mathcal{A}_{p^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow p,k_{i}}+\mathcal{A}^{*}_{p^{\prime\prime},k_{i}\leftarrow p^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}_{p^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\leftarrow p}\right)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\sigma}\{\hat{\delta}_{\Phi}(k^{\prime}_{1}-k_{\sigma_{1}})\cdots\hat{\delta}_{\Phi}(k^{\prime}_{i-1}-k_{\sigma_{i^{\prime}-1}})\hat{\delta}_{\Phi}(k^{\prime}_{i+1}-k_{\sigma_{i^{\prime}+1}})\cdots\hat{\delta}_{\Phi}(k^{\prime}_{n}-k_{\sigma_{n}})\}

\displaystyle+\cdots,

(129)

where the summation of
\sigma
is over
(n-1)!
permutations, and
\hat{\delta}_{\Phi}(k-k^{\prime})=2\omega\hat{\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime})
is the on-shell delta function. Then, the intensity change (
126
) is given by

\displaystyle\bra{\Psi;\beta}\hat{S}^{\dagger}[\hat{N},\hat{S}]\ket{\Psi;\beta}

\displaystyle\simeq\int\differential\Phi(p,p^{\prime},k,k^{\prime})\phi^{*}(p^{\prime})\phi(p)e^{-\|\beta|^{2}}\sum_{n}\frac{\|\beta\|^{2n-2}}{(n-1)!}\beta^{*}(k^{\prime})\beta(k)\left(-\frac{{\rm Disc}_{s}\mathcal{A}_{1^{\prime}\leftarrow 1}}{2\pi i}+\frac{{\rm Disc}_{u}\mathcal{A}_{1^{\prime}\leftarrow 1}}{2\pi i}\right)\hat{\delta}^{(4)}(p+k-p^{\prime}-k^{\prime})+\cdots

\displaystyle=\int\differential\Phi(p,p^{\prime},k,k^{\prime})\phi^{*}(p^{\prime})\phi(p)\beta^{*}(k^{\prime})\beta(k)\left(-\frac{{\rm Disc}_{s}\mathcal{A}_{1^{\prime}\leftarrow 1}}{2\pi i}+\frac{{\rm Disc}_{u}\mathcal{A}_{1^{\prime}\leftarrow 1}}{2\pi i}\right)\hat{\delta}^{(4)}(p+k-p^{\prime}-k^{\prime})+\cdots

(130)

where
{\rm Disc}_{s}
and
{\rm Disc}_{u}
denote the discontinuities associated with the
s
-channel cut (the fist term in (
128
)) and the
u
-channel cut (the second term), respectively.

We choose the initial coherent wave as a partial wave of
\ell
:

\displaystyle\beta(k)=\frac{\sqrt{2\omega}}{4\pi}\gamma(\omega)Y_{\ell,m}(\hat{\bm{k}})\,,\qquad\|\beta\|^{2}=\int\hat{\rm d}\omega\,|\gamma(\omega)|^{2}\,.

(131)

We also assume that classical physics is insensitive to an
\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
change of the initial mass of the macroscopic object

\displaystyle{\rm Disc}_{u}\mathcal{A}_{1^{\prime}\leftarrow 1}\simeq{\rm Disc}_{u}\mathcal{A}_{2^{\prime}\leftarrow 2}\,.

(132)

Then, by taking the classical limit, the intensity change (
126
) is

\displaystyle\bra{\Psi;\beta}\hat{S}^{\dagger}[\hat{N},\hat{S}]\ket{\Psi;\beta}=\int\hat{\rm d}\omega|\gamma(\omega)|^{2}(-P^{\rm abs}_{\ell,m}(\omega)+P^{\rm em}_{\ell,m}(-\omega))+\cdots\,,

(133)

yielding

\displaystyle P^{\rm abs}_{\ell,m}(\omega)-P^{\rm em}_{\ell,m}(-\omega)+\cdots=|\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(\omega)|^{2}\,,

(134)

where
|\mathcal{T}_{\ell}|
is the transmissivity of the partial wave
\ell
.

Now we discuss when the contributions from higher-point amplitudes may be ignored. We stress that we cannot use the expansion in terms of coupling constants, especially for absorption, because we are interested in situations with strong absorption
P^{\rm abs}_{\ell,m}\sim\rho(M_{2})|g|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
. As an example, we consider a two-particle absorption contribution, which is diagrammatically represented by

\displaystyle\hbox to103.32pt{\vbox to43.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.92914pt\lower-25.8073pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{{}{}{{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

(135)

There are
\frac{n(n-1)}{4}\times n!
diagrams of this topology. The correction to the intensity change is

\displaystyle-\int\differential\Phi(p,p^{\prime},k_{1},k_{2},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime})\phi^{*}(p^{\prime})\phi(p)e^{-\|\beta|^{2}}\sum_{n}\frac{1}{2}\frac{\|\beta\|^{2n-4}}{(n-2)!}\beta^{*}(k^{\prime}_{1})\beta^{*}(k^{\prime}_{2})\beta(k_{1})\beta(k_{2})\frac{{\rm Disc}_{s}\mathcal{A}_{6}}{2\pi i}\hat{\delta}^{(4)}(P_{2}-P^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle=-\int\differential\Phi(p,p^{\prime},k_{1},k_{2},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime})\phi^{*}(p^{\prime})\phi(p)\frac{1}{2}\beta^{*}(k^{\prime}_{1})\beta^{*}(k^{\prime}_{2})\beta(k_{1})\beta(k_{2})\frac{{\rm Disc}_{s}\mathcal{A}_{6}}{2\pi i}\hat{\delta}^{(4)}(P_{2}-P^{\prime}_{2})

(136)

where
{\rm Disc}_{s}\mathcal{A}_{6}
is the discontinuity of the six-point amplitude. This contribution is of the order of
\|\beta\|^{4}
and represents a nonlinear correction due to a large intensity of the coherent wave. Therefore, we can ignore the contributions from higher-point absorption amplitudes when the initial intensity of the classical wave is sufficiently small, even if the object exhibits a strong absorption. Note that a similar conclusion does not hold for strong emission. For example, the number of the following mixed induced and spontaneous emission diagrams

n
\vdots
-
+

(137)

is
n\times n!
, which will thus be a correction at
\mathcal{O}(\|\beta\|^{2})
for coherent waves. Hence, neglecting higher-point emission amplitudes requires assuming that emissions are sufficiently weak. All in all, (
20
) holds under the conditions that (i) the initial intensity of the classical wave is small and (ii) emission processes are weak, but it does not require the assumption of weak absorption. This conclusion is consistent with the observation in Ref.
Aoki et al. 2025a
that (
20
) holds well approximately even for a strong absorption regime, corresponding to frequencies
\omega\gtrsim 1/GM
, within the linear perturbation theory around the Schwarzschild BH in both Boulware and Unruh vacuum.
